I just don't have the drive or the peace of mind to actually participate in them right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think if your work situation improved or the stress from the job change was removed, you would regain your interest and enjoyment in activities like paddle-boarding, pickleball, and working on your metal sculptures?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. If they moved me back to my old team or eased up on these quotas, I feel like I could get back to my normal routine and enjoy sculpting, paddle-boarding, and pickleball again within a few months. Right now I just feel stuck, but removing that work stress would make a huge difference.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific times or situations when your feelings of dread, defeat, and worry are significantly worse, such as when you're at work, thinking about work, or when you're trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: They are definitely at their worst during the workday when I'm struggling to meet those repetitive quotas under my new supervisor. It also hits me really hard the moment I get home, where I'm just too wound up and defeated to do anything but stare at my unfinished sculptures."
  }

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Ever since they changed my position at the shop, I just can’t seem to adjust, and it’s throwing off my work and my whole routine. I'm just finding it really hard to get my bearings since the restructuring happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing excessive worry or fear about your new role or work performance since the change?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been dealing with this constant mix of dread and defeat, and I feel so wound up about the new role since the transition. I'm really worried about my performance, especially since my new supervisor already pulled me aside about my slipping numbers and missing some deadlines.\n\nDoctor: Did your feelings of dread, defeat, and worry start immediately after the change in your job position or did they develop gradually over time after the transition?\n\nPatient: They actually started pretty quickly, within a week or two of being switched to the new position and supervisor. Since then, those feelings of dread and defeat have just steadily worsened over the last month instead of settling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant traumatic event or extreme stressor in your life recently, aside from the job change, that you think might be contributing to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: No, there hasn't been anything else like that. I find myself snapping at people and backing out of plans with friends, and my spouse has really noticed how much this is disrupting our household routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your relationships with your spouse and friends have changed since you started feeling this way, and if they've noticed any difference in your behavior or mood?\n\nPatient: My spouse has definitely noticed the change and points out how much this job shift has disrupted our household schedule and my daily functioning. I come home so wound up and defeated that I end up snapping at people or just staring at the couch, and I've been backing out of plans with my friends lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance behaviors, such as avoiding certain tasks or situations at work, or avoiding social interactions with friends and family, as a way of coping with your feelings of dread and defeat?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've definitely been avoiding social interactions and my usual activities. I've backed out of plans with my friends for paddle-boarding and pickleball, and I'm avoiding working on my commissioned metal sculptures. Most nights, I just sit on the couch feeling too exhausted and defeated to do anything I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've stopped doing things you love like paddle-boarding, pickleball, and working on your metal sculptures. Instead of enjoying my hobbies, I end up just sitting on the couch staring at my half-finished sculpture. Right now I just feel stuck, but removing that work stress would make a huge difference.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific times or situations when your feelings of dread, defeat, and worry are significantly worse, such as when you're at work, thinking about work, or when you're trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: They are definitely at their worst during the workday when I'm struggling to meet those repetitive quotas under my new supervisor. It also hits me really hard the moment I get home, where I'm just too wound up and defeated to do anything but stare at my unfinished sculptures.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be experiencing these feelings of dread, defeat, and worry if you were not in this new work situation, or do you believe they would resolve on their own without any changes to your job?\n\nPatient: I definitely wouldn't be feeling this way if it weren't for this new job situation. I really believe that if they moved me back to my old team or eased up on the quotas, I could get back to my normal routine and feel like myself again within a few months. Right now, I just feel completely stuck because of how things are at the shop.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your feelings of dread and defeat are primarily related to your new job situation, but can you think of any other significant life changes or stressors that might be contributing to your emotional state, even if they seem unrelated to your job?"

on the couch staring at my half-finished sculpture instead of doing anything I used to enjoy.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- Symptoms must develop within 3 months of the onset of the     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Ever since they changed my position at the shop, I just can’t seem to adjust, and it’s throwing off my work and my whole routine. I'm just finding it really hard to get my bearings since the restructuring happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing excessive worry or fear about your new role or work performance since the change?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been dealing with this constant mix of dread and defeat, and I feel so wound up about the new role since the transition. I'm really worried about my performance, especially since my new supervisor already pulled me aside about my slipping numbers and missing some deadlines.\n\nDoctor: Did your feelings of dread, defeat, and worry start immediately after the change in your job position or did they develop gradually over time after the transition?\n\nPatient: They actually started pretty quickly, within a week or two of being switched to the new position and supervisor. Since then, those feelings of dread and defeat have just steadily worsened over the last month instead of settling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant traumatic event or extreme stressor in your life recently, aside from the job change, that you think might be contributing to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: No, there hasn't been anything else like that. My life, my routines, and my art sculptures were all going really well until this sudden job restructuring happened last month. This workplace change is the sole reason I've been feeling so thrown off and defeated.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if your feelings of dread and defeat are specifically focused on the new job role and performance, or if they have started to affect other areas of your life beyond work?\n\nPatient: They've definitely spilled over into my personal life; I go home so exhausted and defeated that I just sit on the couch staring at my unfinished metal sculptures. I've stopped doing things I love like paddle-boarding and pickleball, and I'm falling behind on my art commissions and cancelling on friends. It's really starting to disrupt my household and hurt my reputation.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing these feelings of dread, defeat, and worry primarily when you're at work or thinking about work, or have they become more pervasive and started to affect your relationships with friends and family as well?\n\nPatient: They’ve definitely become more pervasive and are affecting my relationships outside of work. I find myself snapping at people and backing out of plans with friends, and my spouse has really noticed how much this is disrupting our household routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your relationships with your spouse and friends have changed since you started feeling this way, and if they've noticed any difference in your behavior or mood?\n\nPatient: My spouse has definitely noticed the change and points out how much this job shift has disrupted our household schedule and my daily functioning. I come home so wound up and defeated that I end up snapping at people or just staring at the couch, and I've been backing out of plans with my friends lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance behaviors, such as avoiding certain tasks or situations at work, or avoiding social interactions with friends and family, as a way of coping with your feelings of dread and defeat?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've definitely been avoiding social interactions and my usual activities. I've backed out of plans with my friends for paddle-boarding and pickleball, and I'm avoiding working on my commissioned metal sculptures. Most nights, I just sit on the couch feeling too exhausted and defeated to do anything I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've stopped doing things you love like paddle-boarding, pickleball, and working on your metal sculptures. Are you finding that you're no longer enjoying these activities or is it more that you're just not having the energy to participate in them?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both, but mostly I’m just too wound up, exhausted, and defeated after work to even try. Instead of enjoying my hobbies, I end up just sitting on the couch staring at my half-finished sculpture. I just don't have the drive or the peace of mind to actually participate in them right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think if your work situation improved or the stress from the job change was removed, you would regain your interest and enjoyment in activities like paddle-boarding, pickleball, and working on your metal sculptures?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. If they moved me back to my old team or eased up on these quotas, I feel like I could get back to my normal routine and enjoy sculpting, paddle-boarding, and pickleball again within a few months. Right now I just feel stuck, but removing that work stress would make a huge difference.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific times or situations when your feelings of dread, defeat, and worry are significantly worse, such as when you're at work, thinking about work, or when you're trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: They are definitely at their worst during the workday when I'm struggling to meet those repetitive quotas under my new supervisor. It also hits me really hard the moment I get home, where I'm just too wound up and defeated to do anything but stare at my unfinished sculptures.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be experiencing these feelings of dread, defeat, and worry if you were not in this new work situation, or do you believe they would resolve on their own without any changes to your job?\n\nPatient: I definitely wouldn't be feeling this way if it weren't for this new job situation. I really believe that if they moved me back to my old team or eased up on the quotas, I could get back to my normal routine and feel like myself again within a few months. Right now, I just feel completely stuck because of how things are at the shop.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your feelings of dread and defeat are primarily related to your new job situation, but can you think of any other significant life changes or stressors that might be contributing to your emotional state, even if they seem unrelated to your job?\n\nPatient: No, there really isn't anything else going on in my life. Before this sudden restructuring at the shop, my home life, my art commissions, and my daily routines were all completely stable and fulfilling. Everything I'm struggling with right now stems directly from this workplace change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing persistent feelings of sadness, emptiness, or hopelessness that are not just limited to your work situation or daily frustrations?"

I find myself snapping at people and backing out of plans with friends, and my spouse has really noticed how much this is disrupting our household routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your relationships with your spouse and friends have changed since you started feeling this way, and if they've noticed any difference in your behavior or mood?\n\nPatient: My spouse has definitely noticed the change and points out how much this job shift has disrupted our household schedule and my daily functioning. Instead of enjoying my hobbies, I end up just sitting on the couch staring at my half-finished sculpture. Right now, I just feel completely stuck because of how things are at the shop.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your feelings of dread and defeat are primarily related to your new job situation, but can you think of any other significant life changes or stressors that might be contributing to your emotional state, even if they seem unrelated to your job?\n\nPatient: No, there really isn't anything else going on in my life. Before this sudden restructuring at the shop, my home life, my art commissions, and my daily routines were all completely stable and fulfilling. Everything I'm struggling with right now stems directly from this workplace change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing persistent feelings of sadness, emptiness, or hopelessness that are not just limited to your work situation or daily frustrations?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say it's a general sadness; these feelings of being on the verge of tears and defeat are directly tied to the sudden job restructuring and how it has disrupted my routine and passion for sculpting. When she began contemplating quitting despite needing the income and her spouse noticed how much the job change was disrupting their household and her once-reliable functioning, she agreed to see a on the couch staring at my half-finished sculpture instead of doing anything I used to enjoy.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- Symptoms must develop within 3 months of the onset of the     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Ever since they changed my position at the shop, I just can’t seem to adjust, and it’s throwing off my work and my whole routine. I'm just finding it really hard to get my bearings since the restructuring happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing excessive worry or fear about your new role or work performance since the change?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been dealing with this constant mix of dread and defeat, and I feel so wound up about the new role since the transition. I'm really worried about my performance, especially since my new supervisor already pulled me aside about my slipping numbers and missing some deadlines.\n\nDoctor: Did your feelings of dread, defeat, and worry start immediately after the change in your job position or did they develop gradually over time after the transition?\n\nPatient: They actually started pretty quickly, within a week or two of being switched to the new position and supervisor. Since then, those feelings of dread and defeat have just steadily worsened over the last month instead of settling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant traumatic event or extreme stressor in your life recently, aside from the job change, that you think might be contributing to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: No, there hasn't been anything else like that. My life, my routines, and my art sculptures were all going really well until this sudden job restructuring happened last month. This workplace change is the sole reason I've been feeling so thrown off and defeated.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if your feelings of dread and defeat are specifically focused on the new job role and performance, or if they have started to affect other areas of your life beyond work?\n\nPatient: They've definitely spilled over into my personal life; I go home so exhausted and defeated that I just sit on the couch staring at my unfinished metal sculptures. I've stopped doing things I love like paddle-boarding and pickleball, and I'm falling behind on my art commissions and cancelling on friends. It's really starting to disrupt my household and hurt my reputation.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing these feelings of dread, defeat, and worry primarily when you're at work or thinking about work, or have they become more pervasive and started to affect your relationships with friends and family as well?\n\nPatient: They’ve definitely become more pervasive and are affecting my relationships outside of work. I find myself snapping at people and backing out of plans with friends, and my spouse has really noticed how much this is disrupting our household routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your relationships with your spouse and friends have changed since you started feeling this way, and if they've noticed any difference in your behavior or mood?\n\nPatient: My spouse has definitely noticed the change and points out how much this job shift has disrupted our household schedule and my daily functioning. I come home so wound up and defeated that I end up snapping at people or just staring at the couch, and I've been backing out of plans with my friends lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance behaviors, such as avoiding certain tasks or situations at work, or avoiding social interactions with friends and family, as a way of coping with your feelings of dread and defeat?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've definitely been avoiding social interactions and my usual activities. I've backed out of plans with my friends for paddle-boarding and pickleball, and I'm avoiding working on my commissioned metal sculptures. Most nights, I just sit on the couch feeling too exhausted and defeated to do anything I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've stopped doing things you love like paddle-boarding, pickleball, and working on your metal sculptures. Are you finding that you're no longer enjoying these activities or is it more that you're just not having the energy to participate in them?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both, but mostly I’m just too wound up, exhausted, and defeated after work to even try. Instead of enjoying my hobbies, I end up just sitting on the couch staring at my half-finished sculpture. I just don't have the drive or the peace of mind to actually participate in them right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think if your work situation improved or the stress from the job change was removed, you would regain your interest and enjoyment in activities like paddle-boarding, pickleball, and working on your metal sculptures?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. If they moved me back to my old team or eased up on these quotas, I feel like I could get back to my normal routine and enjoy sculpting, paddle-boarding, and pickleball again within a few months. Right now I just feel stuck, but removing that work stress would make a huge difference.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific times or situations when your feelings of dread, defeat, and worry are significantly worse, such as when you're at work, thinking about work, or when you're trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: They are definitely at their worst during the workday when I'm struggling to meet those repetitive quotas under my new supervisor. It also hits me really hard the moment I get home, where I'm just too wound up and defeated to do anything but stare at my unfinished sculptures.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be experiencing these feelings of dread, defeat, and worry if you were not in this new work situation, or do you believe they would resolve on their own without any changes to your job?\n\nPatient: I definitely wouldn't be feeling this way if it weren't for this new job situation. I really believe that if they moved me back to my old team or eased up on the quotas, I could get back to my normal routine and feel like myself again within a few months. Right now, I just feel completely stuck because of how things are at the shop.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your feelings of dread and defeat are primarily related to your new job situation, but can you think of any other significant life changes or stressors that might be contributing to your emotional state, even if they seem unrelated to your job?\n\nPatient: No, there really isn't anything else going on in my life. Before this sudden restructuring at the shop, my home life, my art commissions, and my daily routines were all completely stable and fulfilling. Everything I'm struggling with right now stems directly from this workplace change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing persistent feelings of sadness, emptiness, or hopelessness that are not just limited to your work situation or daily frustrations?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say it's a general sadness; these feelings of being on the verge of tears and defeat are directly tied to the sudden job restructuring and how it has disrupted my routine and passion for sculpting. If it weren't for the stress and changes at work, I really don't think I would be feeling this way at all. It all comes back to what's happening at the shop.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any moments of intense emotional reactivity, such as sudden outbursts of anger or tears, in response to relatively minor stressors or reminders of your job situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, ever since they stuck me on this new line, I feel like I’m constantly on the verge of tears or snapping at people. I go home feeling incredibly wound up and defeated, and it really doesn't take much to set me off lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you typically cope with stressful situations, and if there's been a change in your ability to manage stress since the job change occurred?"
  }

Since the job change, my ability to manage stress has completely broken down. Now, I just cancel plans, let my art sit unfinished, and find myself sitting on the couch feeling exhausted and defeated instead of doing the things that used to help me recharge.

The new role emphasized repetitive production work with tighter quotas and less autonomy, and it conflicted with her established routines at home and in her studio, where she usually spent evenings on metal sculptures and weekend gallery visits along the Charleston waterfront. Over the next several weeks, she found herself staying late to meet unfamiliar production targets, cancelling paddle-boarding and pickleball, and letting commissioned art pieces sit half-finished, which strained her relationship with clients and led to pointed feedback from her new boss. When she began contemplating quitting despite needing the income and her spouse noticed how much the job change was disrupting their household and her once-reliable functioning, she agreed to see a just can’t seem to adjust, and it’s throwing off my work and my whole routine.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S081 (Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response): Ever since they stuck me on this new line, I feel like I’m either on the verge of tears or snapping at people, dragging myself through these mind-numbing quotas all day, then going home so wound up and defeated that I just sit on the couch staring at my half-finished sculpture instead of doing anything I used to enjoy.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- Symptoms must develop within 3 months of the onset of the and missing a couple of small deadlines, and at home I’ve backed out of plans with friends and fallen behind on two commissioned sculptures, which is starting to hurt my reputation and our household schedule.\n- duration: This has been going on for about a month now, basically since the company announced the restructuring and shifted me into this new role.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the     "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Ever since they changed my position at the shop, I just can’t seem to adjust, and it’s throwing off my work and my whole routine. I'm just finding it really hard to get my bearings since the restructuring happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing excessive worry or fear about your new role or work performance since the change?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've been dealing with this constant mix of dread and defeat, and I feel so wound up about the new role since the transition. I'm really worried about my performance, especially since my new supervisor already pulled me aside about my slipping numbers and missing some deadlines.\n\nDoctor: Did your feelings of dread, defeat, and worry start immediately after the change in your job position or did they develop gradually over time after the transition?\n\nPatient: They actually started pretty quickly, within a week or two of being switched to the new position and supervisor. Since then, those feelings of dread and defeat have just steadily worsened over the last month instead of settling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant traumatic event or extreme stressor in your life recently, aside from the job change, that you think might be contributing to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: No, there hasn't been anything else like that. My life, my routines, and my art sculptures were all going really well until this sudden job restructuring happened last month. This workplace change is the sole reason I've been feeling so thrown off and defeated.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if your feelings of dread and defeat are specifically focused on the new job role and performance, or if they have started to affect other areas of your life beyond work?\n\nPatient: They've definitely spilled over into my personal life; I go home so exhausted and defeated that I just sit on the couch staring at my unfinished metal sculptures. I've stopped doing things I love like paddle-boarding and pickleball, and I'm falling behind on my art commissions and cancelling on friends. It's really starting to disrupt my household and hurt my reputation.\n\nDoctor: Are you experiencing these feelings of dread, defeat, and worry primarily when you're at work or thinking about work, or have they become more pervasive and started to affect your relationships with friends and family as well?\n\nPatient: They’ve definitely become more pervasive and are affecting my relationships outside of work. I find myself snapping at people and backing out of plans with friends, and my spouse has really noticed how much this is disrupting our household routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your relationships with your spouse and friends have changed since you started feeling this way, and if they've noticed any difference in your behavior or mood?\n\nPatient: My spouse has definitely noticed the change and points out how much this job shift has disrupted our household schedule and my daily functioning. I come home so wound up and defeated that I end up snapping at people or just staring at the couch, and I've been backing out of plans with my friends lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance behaviors, such as avoiding certain tasks or situations at work, or avoiding social interactions with friends and family, as a way of coping with your feelings of dread and defeat?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've definitely been avoiding social interactions and my usual activities. I've backed out of plans with my friends for paddle-boarding and pickleball, and I'm avoiding working on my commissioned metal sculptures. Most nights, I just sit on the couch feeling too exhausted and defeated to do anything I used to enjoy.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned you've stopped doing things you love like paddle-boarding, pickleball, and working on your metal sculptures. Are you finding that you're no longer enjoying these activities or is it more that you're just not having the energy to participate in them?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both, but mostly I’m just too wound up, exhausted, and defeated after work to even try. Instead of enjoying my hobbies, I end up just sitting on the couch staring at my half-finished sculpture. I just don't have the drive or the peace of mind to actually participate in them right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think if your work situation improved or the stress from the job change was removed, you would regain your interest and enjoyment in activities like paddle-boarding, pickleball, and working on your metal sculptures?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. If they moved me back to my old team or eased up on these quotas, I feel like I could get back to my normal routine and enjoy sculpting, paddle-boarding, and pickleball again within a few months. Right now I just feel stuck, but removing that work stress would make a huge difference.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific times or situations when your feelings of dread, defeat, and worry are significantly worse, such as when you're at work, thinking about work, or when you're trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: They are definitely at their worst during the workday when I'm struggling to meet those repetitive quotas under my new supervisor. It also hits me really hard the moment I get home, where I'm just too wound up and defeated to do anything but stare at my unfinished sculptures.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be experiencing these feelings of dread, defeat, and worry if you were not in this new work situation, or do you believe they would resolve on their own without any changes to your job?\n\nPatient: I definitely wouldn't be feeling this way if it weren't for this new job situation. I really believe that if they moved me back to my old team or eased up on the quotas, I could get back to my normal routine and feel like myself again within a few months. Right now, I just feel completely stuck because of how things are at the shop.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your feelings of dread and defeat are primarily related to your new job situation, but can you think of any other significant life changes or stressors that might be contributing to your emotional state, even if they seem unrelated to your job?\n\nPatient: No, there really isn't anything else going on in my life. Before this sudden restructuring at the shop, my home life, my art commissions, and my daily routines were all completely stable and fulfilling. Everything I'm struggling with right now stems directly from this workplace change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing persistent feelings of sadness, emptiness, or hopelessness that are not just limited to your work situation or daily frustrations?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say it's a general sadness; these feelings of being on the verge of tears and defeat are directly tied to the sudden job restructuring and how it has disrupted my routine and passion for sculpting. If it weren't for the stress and changes at work, I really don't think I would be feeling this way at all. It all comes back to what's happening at the shop.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any moments of intense emotional reactivity, such as sudden outbursts of anger or tears, in response to relatively minor stressors or reminders of your job situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, ever since they stuck me on this new line, I feel like I’m constantly on the verge of tears or snapping at people. I go home feeling incredibly wound up and defeated, and it really doesn't take much to set me off lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you typically cope with stressful situations, and if there's been a change in your ability to manage stress since the job change occurred?\n\nPatient: Normally, I cope with stress through creative and active outlets like my metal sculpting, paddle-boarding, and pickleball. Since the job change, my ability to manage stress has completely broken down. Now, I just cancel plans, let my art sit unfinished, and find myself sitting on the couch feeling exhausted and defeated instead of doing the things that used to help me recharge.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any persistent feelings of worthlessness or guilt that are not just related to your job performance, but rather a general feeling about yourself?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't really feel a general sense of worthlessness or guilt about myself as a person. My feelings of defeat and frustration are specifically tied to these job changes and falling behind on my work and art. Outside of this situation, I don't have those constant, general feelings about myself.

It also hits me really hard the moment I get home, where I'm just too wound up and defeated to do anything but stare at my unfinished sculptures.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be experiencing these feelings of dread, defeat, and worry if you were not in this new work situation, or do you believe they would resolve on their own without any changes to your job?\n\nPatient: I definitely wouldn't be feeling this way if it weren't for this new job situation. I really believe that if they moved me back to my old team or eased up on the quotas, I could get back to my normal routine and feel like myself again within a few months. Right now, I just feel completely stuck because of how things are at the shop.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your feelings of dread and defeat are primarily related to your new job situation, but can you think of any other significant life changes or stressors that might be contributing to your emotional state, even if they seem unrelated to your job?\n\nPatient: No, there really isn't anything else going on in my life. Before this sudden restructuring at the shop, my home life, my art commissions, and my daily routines were all completely stable and fulfilling. Everything I'm struggling with right now stems directly from this workplace change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing persistent feelings of sadness, emptiness, or hopelessness that are not just limited to your work situation or daily frustrations?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say it's a general sadness; these feelings of being on the verge of tears and defeat are directly tied to the sudden job restructuring and how it has disrupted my routine and passion for sculpting. If it weren't for the stress and changes at work, I really don't think I would be feeling this way at all. It all comes back to what's happening at the shop.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any moments of intense emotional reactivity, such as sudden outbursts of anger or tears, in response to relatively minor stressors or reminders of your job situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, ever since they stuck me on this new line, I feel like I’m constantly on the verge of tears or snapping at people. I go home feeling incredibly wound up and defeated, and it really doesn't take much to set me off lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you typically cope with stressful situations, and if there's been a change in your ability to manage stress since the job change occurred?\n\nPatient: Normally, I cope with stress through creative and active outlets like my metal sculpting, paddle-boarding, and pickleball. Since the job change, my ability to manage stress has completely broken down. Now, I just cancel plans, let my art sit unfinished, and find myself sitting on the couch feeling exhausted and defeated instead of doing the things that used to help me recharge.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any persistent feelings of worthlessness or guilt that are not just related to your job performance, but rather a general feeling about yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel a general sense of worthlessness or guilt about myself as a person. My feelings of defeat and frustration are specifically tied to these job changes and falling behind on my work and art. Outside of this situation, I don't have those constant, general feelings about myself."

Since then, those feelings of dread and defeat have just steadily worsened over the last month instead of settling down.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant traumatic event or extreme stressor in your life recently, aside from the job change, that you think might be contributing to how you're feeling?\n\nPatient: No, there hasn't been anything else like that. This workplace change is the sole reason I've been feeling so thrown off and defeated.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if your feelings of dread and defeat are specifically focused on the new job role and performance, or if they have started to affect other areas of your life beyond work?\n\nPatient: They've definitely spilled over into my personal life; I go home so exhausted and defeated that I just sit on the couch staring at my unfinished metal sculptures. I've stopped doing things I love like paddle-boarding and pickleball, and I'm falling behind on my art commissions and cancelling on friends. I find myself snapping at people and backing out of plans with friends, and my spouse has really noticed how much this is disrupting our household routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your relationships with your spouse and friends have changed since you started feeling this way, and if they've noticed any difference in your behavior or mood?\n\nPatient: My spouse has definitely noticed the change and points out how much this job shift has disrupted our household schedule and my daily functioning. I come home so wound up and defeated that I end up snapping at people or just staring at the couch, and I've been backing out of plans with my friends lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant avoidance behaviors, such as avoiding certain tasks or situations at work, or avoiding social interactions with friends and family, as a way of coping with your feelings of dread and defeat?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've definitely been avoiding social interactions and my usual activities. If they moved me back to my old team or eased up on these quotas, I feel like I could get back to my normal routine and enjoy sculpting, paddle-boarding, and pickleball again within a few months. Right now I just feel stuck, but removing that work stress would make a huge difference.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific times or situations when your feelings of dread, defeat, and worry are significantly worse, such as when you're at work, thinking about work, or when you're trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: They are definitely at their worst during the workday when I'm struggling to meet those repetitive quotas under my new supervisor. It also hits me really hard the moment I get home, where I'm just too wound up and defeated to do anything but stare at my unfinished sculptures.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be experiencing these feelings of dread, defeat, and worry if you were not in this new work situation, or do you believe they would resolve on their own without any changes to your job?\n\nPatient: I definitely wouldn't be feeling this way if it weren't for this new job situation. I really believe that if they moved me back to my old team or eased up on the quotas, I could get back to my normal routine and feel like myself again within a few months. Right now, I just feel completely stuck because of how things are at the shop.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your feelings of dread and defeat are primarily related to your new job situation, but can you think of any other significant life changes or stressors that might be contributing to your emotional state, even if they seem unrelated to your job?\n\nPatient: No, there really isn't anything else going on in my life. Before this sudden restructuring at the shop, my home life, my art commissions, and my daily routines were all completely stable and fulfilling. Everything I'm struggling with right now stems directly from this workplace change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing persistent feelings of sadness, emptiness, or hopelessness that are not just limited to your work situation or daily frustrations?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say it's a general sadness; these feelings of being on the verge of tears and defeat are directly tied to the sudden job restructuring and how it has disrupted my routine and passion for sculpting. If it weren't for the stress and changes at work, I really don't think I would be feeling this way at all. It all comes back to what's happening at the shop.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any moments of intense emotional reactivity, such as sudden outbursts of anger or tears, in response to relatively minor stressors or reminders of your job situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, ever since they stuck me on this new line, I feel like I’m constantly on the verge of tears or snapping at people. I go home feeling incredibly wound up and defeated, and it really doesn't take much to set me off lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you typically cope with stressful situations, and if there's been a change in your ability to manage stress since the job change occurred?\n\nPatient: Normally, I cope with stress through creative and active outlets like my metal sculpting, paddle-boarding, and pickleball. Since the job change, my ability to manage stress has completely broken down. Now, I just cancel plans, let my art sit unfinished, and find myself sitting on the couch feeling exhausted and defeated instead of doing the things that used to help me recharge.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any persistent feelings of worthlessness or guilt that are not just related to your job performance, but rather a general feeling about yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel a general sense of worthlessness or guilt about myself as a person. My feelings of defeat and frustration are specifically tied to these job changes and falling behind on my work and art. Outside of this situation, I don't have those constant, general feelings about myself.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that you've lost interest in activities you used to enjoy, such as paddle-boarding and working on your metal sculptures. Can you tell me if there's been a complete loss of pleasure in these activities or if you still feel some enjoyment when you do manage to participate in them?\n\nPatient: I haven't actually been participating in those activities at all lately because when I get home, I feel too wound up and defeated to even start. Looking at my half-finished sculptures or thinking about paddle-boarding just makes me feel stressed and guilty about falling behind. It doesn't bring me any pleasure right now; it just adds to the pressure."

Instead of enjoying my hobbies, I end up just sitting on the couch staring at my half-finished sculpture. I just don't have the drive or the peace of mind to actually participate in them right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you think if your work situation improved or the stress from the job change was removed, you would regain your interest and enjoyment in activities like paddle-boarding, pickleball, and working on your metal sculptures?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. Right now I just feel stuck, but removing that work stress would make a huge difference.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there are any specific times or situations when your feelings of dread, defeat, and worry are significantly worse, such as when you're at work, thinking about work, or when you're trying to fall asleep?\n\nPatient: They are definitely at their worst during the workday when I'm struggling to meet those repetitive quotas under my new supervisor. It also hits me really hard the moment I get home, where I'm just too wound up and defeated to do anything but stare at my unfinished sculptures.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be experiencing these feelings of dread, defeat, and worry if you were not in this new work situation, or do you believe they would resolve on their own without any changes to your job?\n\nPatient: I definitely wouldn't be feeling this way if it weren't for this new job situation. Right now, I just feel completely stuck because of how things are at the shop.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your feelings of dread and defeat are primarily related to your new job situation, but can you think of any other significant life changes or stressors that might be contributing to your emotional state, even if they seem unrelated to your job?\n\nPatient: No, there really isn't anything else going on in my life. Everything I'm struggling with right now stems directly from this workplace change.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing persistent feelings of sadness, emptiness, or hopelessness that are not just limited to your work situation or daily frustrations?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say it's a general sadness; these feelings of being on the verge of tears and defeat are directly tied to the sudden job restructuring and how it has disrupted my routine and passion for sculpting. If it weren't for the stress and changes at work, I really don't think I would be feeling this way at all. It all comes back to what's happening at the shop.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any moments of intense emotional reactivity, such as sudden outbursts of anger or tears, in response to relatively minor stressors or reminders of your job situation?\n\nPatient: Yes, ever since they stuck me on this new line, I feel like I’m constantly on the verge of tears or snapping at people. I go home feeling incredibly wound up and defeated, and it really doesn't take much to set me off lately.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how you typically cope with stressful situations, and if there's been a change in your ability to manage stress since the job change occurred?\n\nPatient: Normally, I cope with stress through creative and active outlets like my metal sculpting, paddle-boarding, and pickleball. Since the job change, my ability to manage stress has completely broken down. Now, I just cancel plans, let my art sit unfinished, and find myself sitting on the couch feeling exhausted and defeated instead of doing the things that used to help me recharge.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any persistent feelings of worthlessness or guilt that are not just related to your job performance, but rather a general feeling about yourself?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really feel a general sense of worthlessness or guilt about myself as a person. My feelings of defeat and frustration are specifically tied to these job changes and falling behind on my work and art. If they moved me back to my old fabrication team or eased up on these quotas, I feel confident I'd get back to my normal routine and passion for metal sculpting within a few months instead of feeling so stuck."

Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
